> > **Priority: Medium.** A deeper treatment of the neural substrates of memory: long-term potentiation (LTP) as the cellular mechanism of synaptic consolidation, the hippocampal-neocortical architecture and its role in consolidation, place cells and grid cells, the amygdala's role in emotional memory enhancement, and the current state of the engram literature (the search for the physical substrate of individual memories). This node would provide the neuroscientific grounding for mechanisms described in the current report at a more functional level, and would connect to the philosophy of mind literature on memory and personal identity.

> [!further-exploration] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **Deepening Your Practice**
> 
> 

> [!topic-idea] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **[[Situation-Models-in-Text-Comprehension]]**
> 
> A dedicated foundational report on this topic would provide comprehensive treatment of [[Walter-Kintsch]]'s Construction-Integration model, [[Rolf-Zwaan]]'s Event Indexing Model, and the multidimensional nature of situation model updating during reading. It would cover empirical methods (reading-time paradigms, recognition probes, inference verification) and connect to educational applications in reading comprehension instruction, particularly the distinction between strategies targeting text-base representation (summarising, rereading) and strategies targeting situation model quality (elaborative questioning, prediction tasks). Priority: **High** — foundational for understanding literate cognition.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **[[Conceptual-Change-Theory]]**
> 
> A foundational report on [[Stella-Vosniadou]]'s framework-theory approach and its successors (including Chi's "knowledge-in-pieces" alternative). The report would cover mechanisms of conceptual change (accretion, restructuring, revision), empirical documentation of misconceptions across science domains, instructional interventions that succeed and fail, and the relationship between conceptual change and motivation (why students resist model revision even when presented with disconfirming evidence). Priority: **High** — essential for science educators and instructional designers.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **[[Multimedia-Learning]]**
> 
> A foundational treatment of [[Richard-Mayer]]'s Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning as the most systematically developed instructional application of the mental models framework. The report would cover the dual-channel assumption, cognitive load as constraint on model construction, the twelve evidence-based principles of multimedia design (coherence, signalling, segmenting, etc.), and critical discussion of boundary conditions and replication concerns in the multimedia learning literature. Priority: **High** — immediately applicable to instructional design practice.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **[[Counterfactual-Reasoning]]**
> 
> A foundational treatment of [[Ruth-Byrne]]'s suppositional theory of counterfactuals, including the dual-model account (actual vs. imagined), the mutability principle (some facts are more easily mentally "undone" than others), and applications to regret, blame attribution, causal judgment, and legal reasoning. The report would engage with alternative accounts (simulation theory, possible worlds semantics) and discuss educational implications for teaching causal and hypothetical reasoning. Priority: **Medium**.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **[[Spatial Mental Models]]**
> 
> A foundational report covering mental rotation (Shepard & Cooper), spatial reasoning from text and diagrams (Hegarty), and the role of spatial ability in model-based reasoning across domains. It would address gender and individual differences in spatial reasoning, interventions that improve spatial model quality, and implications for STEM instruction where spatial model construction is critical (organic chemistry, anatomy, physics). Priority: **Medium**.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **[[Analogical-Reasoning]]**
> 
> A foundational treatment of [[Dedre-Gentner]]'s structure-mapping theory and its convergence with the mental models framework: analogical reasoning as the mapping of relational structure from a source domain to a target domain — in effect, using a familiar model as a scaffold for constructing a new model of an unfamiliar situation. The report would cover empirical work on analogy comprehension and production, the role of analogy in scientific discovery (Nersessian), and instructional applications of analogy in conceptual explanation. Priority: **Medium**.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11|mental-models-johnson-laird-foundational-report-2026-03-11]]
> **[[Mental-Simulation]]**
> 
> A foundational report addressing the relationship between mental models and embodied simulation, drawing on [[Lawrence-Barsalou]]'s Perceptual Symbol Systems as a mechanistic account of how models are "run". The report would cover the broader embodied cognition literature, grounding effects in language comprehension, and action-oriented views of representation — examining whether mental model theory and embodied simulation theories are rival accounts or complementary descriptions at different levels of analysis. Priority: **Medium**.

> [!further-exploration] From: [[metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-and-critical-thinking-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **Deepening Your Practice**
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Metacognitive-Scaffolding-Design-Patterns]]
> > A focused treatment of the specific instructional design patterns — think-aloud protocols, structured reflection prompts, metacognitive journals, peer critique frameworks, and self-assessment rubrics — that have the strongest evidence base for developing metacognitive capacity. Would include detailed templates, worked examples, and design principles for embedding these patterns into courses across domains. The primary value-add beyond this foundational report: specific, implementable tools for instructional practice.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Critical Thinking Assessment and Measurement]]
> > A rigorous examination of the major psychometric instruments for critical thinking assessment — the Watson-Glaser Critical Thinking Appraisal, the Halpern Critical Thinking Assessment, the Collegiate Learning Assessment Plus (CLA+), the California Critical Thinking Skills Test — including their theoretical grounding, validity evidence, practical utility, and limitations. Also addresses classroom-based rubric design for critical thinking assessment. Essential for anyone responsible for evaluating the effectiveness of critical thinking instruction.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Epistemic Cognition and Reflective Judgment]]
> > A deep dive into the King-Kitchener Reflective Judgment Model and the broader research tradition on epistemic cognition — how individuals understand the nature of knowledge, certainty, and justification. Would cover the seven developmental stages in detail, the longitudinal evidence for stage progression, the implications for instruction at different educational levels, and the relationship between reflective judgment and performance on ill-structured reasoning tasks.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Socratic Questioning Techniques in Education]]
> > A treatment of the structure, taxonomy, and evidence base for Socratic questioning as a pedagogical method — including the distinction between elenctic and maieutic questioning, the taxonomy of Socratic question types (conceptual clarification, probing assumptions, probing evidence, questioning viewpoints, probing implications, meta-questions), and research on the conditions under which Socratic dialogue produces genuine critical thinking development versus merely eliciting performance.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Transfer of Learning Across Domains]]
> > The cognitive science of transfer, with particular application to metacognitive and critical thinking skills. Would cover the theoretical landscape (identical elements theory, schema theory, preparation for future learning), the empirical evidence on far transfer, the design conditions that support or inhibit transfer, and the implications for curriculum sequencing and instructional design. This is arguably the most important expansion topic for practitioners who want their instruction to have lasting impact.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Intellectual Virtues and Disposition Theory]]
> > A treatment of virtue epistemology and its application to education — the cultivation of intellectual humility, intellectual courage, intellectual tenacity, open-mindedness, and fair-mindedness as educational goals. Would draw on the philosophical tradition of virtue epistemology (Jason Baehr, Linda Zagzebski), the psychological research on intellectual dispositions and their predictors, and the educational literature on character formation and intellectual development. Addresses the "disposition problem" identified in Phase VI of this report.

> [!further-exploration] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **Deepening Your Practice**
> 
> 

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Retrieval-Practice-and-the-Testing-Effect]]**
> 
> A full treatment of the empirical science behind retrieval practice — the most robustly supported learning strategy in cognitive psychology. Would cover the mechanisms of the testing effect (retrieval practice strengthens memory traces and enhances metacognitive calibration simultaneously), the conditions under which it is most and least effective, practical protocols for implementing it across different content types, and its integration with spaced repetition systems. Priority: High — the testing effect is perhaps the single most actionable finding from the metacognitive research program.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Spaced-Repetition-Systems]]**
> 
> An exploration of how the spacing effect — one of the most replicable findings in memory research — can be systematically implemented through scheduling algorithms. Would cover the history from Ebbinghaus through modern SRS applications, the theory of the spacing effect, practical implementation using tools like Anki or SuperMemo, and the specific metacognitive skills required to maintain an SRS effectively over time. Priority: High — SRS represents the most powerful practical application of metacognitive learning science currently available.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[The Science of Deliberate Practice]]**
> 
> A comprehensive treatment of Anders Ericsson's research program on expert skill acquisition. Would cover the empirical evidence distinguishing deliberate practice from experience and play, the role of mental representations in expert performance, the design principles of effective deliberate practice, and the implications for professional development across domains including music, sports, medicine, and knowledge work. Priority: High — deliberate practice is the operational metacognitive framework for professional skill development.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Cognitive Biases and Their Impact on Self-Assessment]]**
> 
> A systematic survey of how cognitive biases distort metacognitive accuracy, covering the Dunning-Kruger effect in depth, overconfidence effects in expert judgment, hindsight bias and its corruption of learning from experience, confirmation bias in self-assessment, and practical debiasing strategies. Would address both the empirical debates about some of these biases and their practical significance for lifelong learning. Priority: Medium — essential for understanding calibration failures but somewhat specialized.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Metacognitive Interventions in Education]]**
> 
> A research synthesis of empirically tested pedagogical approaches for developing metacognitive skills in learners across developmental stages, including reciprocal teaching, self-questioning protocols, learning journals, think-aloud instruction, and the PISA research on metacognitive strategy use. Would connect theory to practice for educators and curriculum designers. Priority: Medium — particularly relevant for those in teaching or instructional design roles.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Vygotsky and Socio-Cultural Learning Theory]]**
> 
> A full treatment of Vygotsky's theoretical framework and its contemporary applications, covering the Zone of Proximal Development, the role of cultural tools and language in mediating higher cognitive functions, the concept of internalization, and the social origins of self-regulation. Would extend the genealogical treatment in Phase II and connect to contemporary research on peer learning, collaborative cognition, and the social dimensions of professional expertise. Priority: Medium — provides important theoretical depth for the sociogenic view of metacognitive development.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-lifelong-learning-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Personal Learning Architecture Design]]**
> 
> A practical synthesis of how to design a deliberate personal learning system that integrates the metacognitive principles covered in this report — including goal-setting frameworks, deliberate practice protocols, spaced repetition systems, reflective journaling practices, and feedback cultivation strategies — into a coherent and sustainable personal development architecture. Priority: High — the actionable culmination of the theoretical program; particularly relevant for knowledge workers and professionals engaged in continuous self-development.

> [!further-exploration] From: [[metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **Deepening Your Practice**
> 
> 

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Metacognitive Feelings: Affect, Fluency, and Learning Judgments]]**
> 
> A deep-dive into Efklides' work on the phenomenological dimension of metacognition — how Feelings of Knowing, Ease of Learning, Judgments of Learning, and Feelings of Non-fluency constitute a semi-autonomous layer of self-monitoring that carries affective content. This report would examine calibration research, the systematic biases that plague metacognitive judgments (the illusion of knowing, overclaiming), and the specific conditions under which metacognitive feelings provide reliable vs. unreliable diagnostic information. Critical for anyone working in formative assessment, learning analytics, or reflective practice design.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Designing-Autonomy-Supportive-Learning-Environments]]**
> 
> Applied treatment of the SDT-derived principles for structuring educational and training contexts to support autonomous motivation and metacognitive engagement simultaneously. Would cover empirical evidence on autonomy-supportive vs. controlling instructor behavior, structural design choices (assessment design, choice provision, rationale-giving), and the specific challenge of maintaining autonomy support in high-stakes accountability contexts. Practical toolkit orientation with evidence base for each design principle.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Self-Efficacy-Theory-and-Academic-Achievement]]**
> 
> Bandura's full social cognitive framework applied to educational contexts — tracing the four sources of self-efficacy (mastery experience, vicarious modeling, social persuasion, physiological interpretation) and their differential effectiveness in building durable vs. fragile academic self-efficacy. Would examine the self-efficacy → metacognitive depth pathway in detail, with attention to domain-specificity, developmental trajectories, and targeted intervention strategies for low-self-efficacy learners.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Goal-Orientation-Theory-in-Educational-Contexts]]**
> 
> A focused treatment of mastery vs. performance goal orientations — their measurement, developmental origins, contextual determinants, and differential consequences for metacognitive engagement and motivational sustainability. Would incorporate the 2×2 framework (Elliot & McGregor, 2001) distinguishing performance-approach from performance-avoidance, examine motivational climate research (Ames), and address the practical question of how goal framing in instructional language shapes learner psychology over time.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Flow-Theory-and-Optimal-Learning-Experiences]]**
> 
> Csikszentmihalyi's flow framework as a limiting case of intrinsic motivation and metacognitive transparency — examining how the challenge-skill balance produces states of absorbed engagement where metacognitive monitoring becomes largely implicit. Would connect flow conditions to learning design, examining how deliberately constructed experiences of appropriate challenge can build both intrinsic motivation and metacognitive repertoire simultaneously.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05|metacognition-motivation-foundational-report-2026-03-05]]
> **[[Attribution-Theory-and-Learning-Resilience]]**
> 
> Weiner's attributional framework in depth — examining how the dimensions of locus, stability, and controllability interact to produce adaptive vs. maladaptive responses to academic failure. Would address the specific mechanism by which attribution retraining interventions (attributing failure to controllable, unstable factors) break vicious cycle dynamics and restore metacognitive effort investment in previously defeated learners.

> [!further-exploration] From: [[political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06|political-reasoning-decision-making-foundational-report-2026-03-06]]
> **Deepening Your Practice**
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Partisan-Identity-and-Motivated-Reasoning]]
> > A full-length foundational report on this topic would provide the most detailed mechanistic treatment of how social identity drives motivated political cognition. It would cover Lodge and Taber's experimental program in depth, Kahan's "identity-protective cognition" research, Huddy's social identity approach to partisanship, and the growing literature on "affective polarization" as distinct from ideological polarization. The key question for the knowledge base: under what conditions does partisan identity override accuracy motivation entirely, and what structural or developmental factors moderate this? Priority: **high** — directly extends this report's core treatment.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Affective Intelligence Theory — Full Treatment]]
> > The treatment of affective intelligence in this report is introductory. A full foundational report would provide the theoretical architecture of the disposition and surveillance systems, the neurological correlates of the distinction, the specific evidence from NES-based studies, extensions to non-electoral political contexts (e.g., attitudes toward immigration, environmental threats), and the design implications for political communication. The critical question: does the anxiety-deliberation link hold cross-culturally? Priority: **high** — one of the most underexplored frameworks in the knowledge base.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Media Literacy and Disinformation Resistance]]
> > A dedicated report on this topic would synthesize the inoculation theory literature, the SIFT and lateral reading evidence, the broader media literacy intervention research (including its contested efficacy record), and the platform governance literature on algorithmic amplification of misinformation. The key practical question: what combination of individual cognitive training and structural information environment design produces the most durable resistance to political misinformation? Priority: **high** — immediate practical relevance in current information environment.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Deliberative Democracy and Epistemic Citizenship]]
> > The normative literature on what democratic deliberation should look like and the empirical literature on what it actually produces when well-designed (Fishkin's Deliberative Polling, OECD's work on citizens' assemblies, Deliberative Mini-Publics research) are the foundation for this report. It would address the tension between epistemic and participatory democratic values, the evidence on whether deliberative institutions scale, and the pedagogical implications for civic education. Priority: **high** — essential complement to this report's practical conclusions.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Heuristics in Voting Behavior]]
> > Lupia's "shortcuts" model, Popkin's "reasoning voter," the party-cue literature, and the retrospective voting research all form the empirical core of this report. The central theoretical question — whether heuristic voting approximates fully-informed voting and under what conditions — is both normatively significant for democratic theory and practically significant for electoral system design. Priority: **medium** — important extension of Phase IV's mechanism analysis.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Epistemic-Calibration]]
> > Extending Tetlock's superforecaster research into a full treatment of what well-calibrated political judgment is, how it is developed, and how individual differences in calibration interact with political context. Would connect the performance psychology literature on deliberate practice with the political reasoning literature. Priority: **medium** — directly extends the knowledge base's existing treatment of metacognition.
> 
> > [!topic-idea] [[Cross-Cultural Variation in Political Reasoning]]
> > A critical examination of the WEIRD-sample problem in political psychology: which mechanisms have been documented outside Western democratic contexts, which appear culture-specific, and what cross-national comparative work exists on political reasoning quality. Directly addresses the most significant scope limitation of the present report. Priority: **medium** — important for epistemic integrity of the knowledge base.

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Memory Systems — Working Memory and Long-Term Memory ✅
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive survey of the human memory architecture, tracing the development of multi-store models from Atkinson-Shiffrin through Baddeley's working memory model to modern consolidation accounts. The report maps the distinct subsystems — phonological loop, visuospatial sketchpad, central executive, and episodic buffer — alongside long-term memory's declarative and non-declarative branches, grounding each in neuroscientific evidence and examining the functional interplay that underlies all learning. Historical debates, current consensus positions, and practical implications for knowledge acquisition are treated with equal depth.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Multi-store model, working memory capacity, phonological loop, episodic and semantic memory, procedural memory, memory consolidation, synaptic plasticity, limited-capacity processing, retrieval, encoding
>     - **Related Topics**: Cognitive Load Theory, Schema Theory, Retrieval Practice (Testing Effect), Levels of Processing Theory, Neuroscience of Memory Consolidation

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Memory Systems — Working Memory and Long-Term Memory
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A precise analytical investigation into working memory capacity constraints and their downstream effects on learning, skill acquisition, and expertise development. The report examines the debate over capacity limits (Miller's 7±2 versus Cowan's ~4 chunks), the mechanisms by which long-term memory schemas offload working memory burden, and the implications of this relationship for instructional sequencing, study strategy selection, and the design of cognitively demanding tasks. Special attention is given to individual differences in working memory span and their predictive validity across academic domains.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Working memory capacity limits, chunking, schema offloading, phonological loop interference, cognitive bottleneck, individual differences, span tasks, reading comprehension, mathematical reasoning
>     - **Related Topics**: Cognitive Load Theory, Schema Theory, Chunking and Expertise Development, Dual Coding Theory, Retrieval Practice

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Schema Theory ✅
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: An encyclopedic treatment of schema theory from Bartlett's constructivist roots through Rumelhart and Norman's connectionist elaborations to contemporary applications in instructional design and knowledge management. The report explains how schemas function as cognitive frameworks that organize prior knowledge, guide perception and comprehension, support inference generation, and determine what is retained or distorted during encoding and recall. Applications across reading comprehension, problem solving, expertise acquisition, and curriculum design are examined alongside theoretical critiques.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Knowledge structures, assimilation, accommodation, prior knowledge activation, top-down processing, inference, script theory, frame theory, conceptual change, novice-to-expert development
>     - **Related Topics**: Constructivist Learning Theory, Cognitive Load Theory, Advance Organizers, Prior Knowledge Activation in Instruction, Transfer of Learning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Schema Theory
>   - **Report Type**: First Principles
>     - **Description**: A first-principles deconstruction of schema theory that interrogates its foundational assumptions — particularly the claim that knowledge is organized in discrete, retrievable packets rather than as distributed activation patterns — and examines the tension between schema-based and exemplar-based theories of memory. The analysis audits the assumption that schemas are passive containers versus active constructive mechanisms, traces the philosophical debt to Kantian categories of understanding, and attempts to reconstruct a minimal, axiom-grounded account of how organized prior knowledge influences new learning. Convergences with connectionist models and divergences from modular cognitive architectures are carefully marked.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Knowledge representation, cognitive unit debate, top-down vs. bottom-up processing, constructivist memory, distributed representation, prototype vs. exemplar, Kantian categories, conceptual change mechanisms
>     - **Related Topics**: Constructivist Learning Theory, Levels of Processing Theory, Mental Models (Johnson-Laird), Embodied Cognition, Dual Process Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Levels of Processing Theory (Craik & Lockhart)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A focused analytical examination of Craik and Lockhart's depth-of-processing framework, which proposed that memory strength is a byproduct of encoding elaboration rather than rehearsal duration or structural storage. The report critically analyzes the original 1972 model, the challenges posed by the circularity objection, Craik and Tulving's elaboration studies, and how the framework has evolved through transfer-appropriate processing and encoding specificity. The analysis evaluates the theory's explanatory power relative to the multi-store model and assesses its ongoing relevance to study strategy research.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Shallow vs. deep encoding, semantic elaboration, structural vs. phonemic vs. semantic processing, transfer-appropriate processing, distinctiveness, orthographic-phonological-semantic hierarchy, rehearsal types
>     - **Related Topics**: Memory Systems, Encoding Specificity Principle, Retrieval Practice, Self-Explanation, Elaborative Interrogation

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Dual Coding Theory (Paivio)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A rigorous analytical treatment of Paivio's dual coding theory, which posits two distinct but interconnected symbolic systems — verbal and imagistic — that operate in parallel and mutually activate one another through referential connections. The report examines the empirical basis for the theory (concreteness effects, imagery mnemonics, picture superiority), its relationship to Baddeley's multicomponent working memory model, and its instantiation in Mayer's cognitive theory of multimedia learning. Instructional implications for visual-verbal integration, note-taking, and diagram use in learning materials are systematically addressed.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Verbal and imagistic systems, logogens and imagens, referential connections, picture superiority effect, concreteness, mnemonics, redundancy effect, multimedia design principles
>     - **Related Topics**: Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning, Sketch-Noting and Visual Note-Taking, Cognitive Load Theory, Memory Systems, Dual Coding in Practice

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Transfer of Learning
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive foundational report on transfer of learning — the phenomenon by which knowledge, skills, and cognitive dispositions acquired in one context influence performance in new, structurally related or distant contexts. The report maps the classic taxonomy (near vs. far, positive vs. negative, specific vs. general), surveys the major theoretical accounts from identical elements theory through Gick and Holyoak's analogical transfer model, and examines the conditions — contextual variability, interleaving, abstract principle instruction — that maximize far transfer. The persistent "transfer problem" in education and its implications for curriculum design are treated at depth.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Near and far transfer, positive and negative transfer, analogical transfer, abstract principles, identical elements, contextual variability, encoding variability, surface vs. structural similarity, generalization
>     - **Related Topics**: Schema Theory, Problem-Based Learning, Interleaving, Desirable Difficulties, Expert vs. Novice Problem Solving

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Forgetting Curves and Interference Theory
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: An analytical deep-dive into Ebbinghaus's forgetting curve as an empirical phenomenon and the theoretical accounts that explain it — decay theory, proactive and retroactive interference, retrieval failure, and motivated forgetting. The report traces Ebbinghaus's original methodological innovations, examines how subsequent interference research by McGeoch, Underwood, and others displaced decay theory as the dominant account, and connects these findings to the modern spacing effect literature and the practical rationale for spaced repetition systems. The analysis evaluates what the forgetting curve does and does not tell us about memory's functional architecture.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Ebbinghaus savings method, exponential decay function, proactive interference, retroactive interference, retrieval failure, motivated forgetting, spacing effect, forgetting rate individual differences
>     - **Related Topics**: Spaced Practice (Distributed Practice), Retrieval Practice, Memory Systems, Spaced Repetition Systems (Anki), Desirable Difficulties

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Chunking and Expertise Development
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: An analytical examination of Miller's chunking concept and Chase and Simon's landmark chess studies, exploring how the progressive aggregation of lower-level elements into higher-order meaningful units is the cognitive engine driving expertise. The report traces how expert chunking enables pattern recognition, reduces working memory load, and supports rapid solution generation — and how this mechanism links to deliberate practice, long-term working memory theory (Ericsson & Kintsch), and the conditions under which chunked knowledge transfers to novel domains. Implications for instruction, sequencing, and the role of meaningful context in knowledge acquisition are fully developed.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Chunk formation, pattern recognition, working memory offloading, expert-novice differences, long-term working memory, perceptual learning, deliberate practice, template theory
>     - **Related Topics**: Memory Systems, Deliberate Practice, Cognitive Load Theory, Schema Theory, Expert vs. Novice Problem Solving

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Cognitive Load Theory ✅
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive foundational survey of Sweller's Cognitive Load Theory — one of the most empirically productive frameworks in educational psychology — from its origins in the late 1980s to its contemporary multi-component formulation. The report explains the distinction between intrinsic, extraneous, and germane cognitive load; the role of element interactivity in determining intrinsic difficulty; and the evidence base for instructional effects including the worked example, split-attention, redundancy, expertise reversal, and modality effects. Theoretical extensions, critiques, and integration with schema theory and adaptive instruction are treated with scholarly depth.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Intrinsic load, extraneous load, germane load, element interactivity, worked example effect, split-attention effect, expertise reversal effect, modality effect, redundancy effect, schema formation
>     - **Related Topics**: Memory Systems, Schema Theory, Instructional Design, Dual Coding Theory, Merrill's First Principles of Instruction

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Cognitive Load Theory
>   - **Report Type**: First Principles
>     - **Description**: A first-principles interrogation of Cognitive Load Theory's foundational assumptions, beginning with its debt to the multi-store memory model and examining whether the tripartite load taxonomy (intrinsic/extraneous/germane) is empirically distinguishable or operationally circular. The analysis audits the assumption that working memory is the sole processing bottleneck, questions the parsimony of treating all learning-irrelevant mental effort as equally harmful, and examines whether the theory adequately accounts for the role of motivation, emotion, and prior knowledge in modulating effective processing. Reconstruction proposes a minimal set of axioms — limited working memory capacity, schema-based long-term memory, and adaptive memory architecture — that preserve the theory's predictive power with greater conceptual clarity.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Working memory bottleneck assumption, load distinguishability problem, element interactivity operationalization, adaptive memory architecture, circular load attribution, motivation-cognition interaction, schema sufficiency
>     - **Related Topics**: Memory Systems, Dual Process Theory, Desirable Difficulties, Schema Theory, Metacognition in Learning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Dual Process Theory (System 1 / System 2)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on dual process theories of cognition — primarily Kahneman's System 1 / System 2 formulation but contextualized within the broader tradition from Epstein's cognitive-experiential self-theory and Sloman's associative-rule-based model. The report maps the defining properties of each system (speed, automaticity, effort, consciousness, accuracy), surveys the empirical phenomena that motivate the dual-process distinction (cognitive biases, heuristics, intuition-versus-deliberation trade-offs), and examines how the framework applies to learning, reasoning, moral judgment, and expertise development. Current debates about whether a single-process architecture is sufficient are addressed with intellectual evenhandedness.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Automatic vs. controlled processing, heuristics, intuition, deliberative reasoning, cognitive effort, debiasing, default intervention model, type 1 and type 2 processes, metacognitive control
>     - **Related Topics**: Heuristics and Biases, Executive Function and Cognitive Control, Metacognition in Learning, Cognitive Load Theory, Bayesian Reasoning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Dual Process Theory (System 1 / System 2)
>   - **Report Type**: First Principles
>     - **Description**: A rigorous first-principles audit of dual process theory that interrogates the central assumption of two architecturally distinct processing systems, examining whether the vast empirical literature might be parsimoniously explained by a single system operating under varying resource or time constraints. The analysis deconstructs the properties typically attributed to System 1 — automaticity, unconsciousness, fast — and examines whether these attributes cluster reliably or fragment under empirical scrutiny. Axioms are derived about the functional relationship between processing fluency and confidence, between familiarity and perceived validity, and between cognitive load and error rates, yielding a reconstruction that preserves the theory's practical utility while clarifying its ontological commitments.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Processing system individuation, automaticity decomposition, fluency-confidence coupling, single-system alternatives, load-error relationship, dual-task methodology limitations, default intervention debate
>     - **Related Topics**: Heuristics and Biases (Kahneman & Tversky), Executive Function, Metacognition, Cognitive Biases and Debiasing Strategies, Bounded Rationality

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Executive Function and Cognitive Control
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive foundational treatment of executive function — the family of higher-order cognitive control processes that regulate goal-directed behavior, including inhibitory control, working memory updating, cognitive flexibility, planning, and monitoring. The report traces the historical development of the construct from Luria's frontal lobe work through Miyake's unity-and-diversity model, examines the neural underpinnings in prefrontal and anterior cingulate cortex, surveys developmental trajectories from childhood through aging, and addresses both the predictive validity of executive function for academic outcomes and interventions designed to support executive function development.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Inhibitory control, working memory updating, cognitive flexibility, unity-and-diversity model, prefrontal cortex, task switching costs, goal maintenance, planning, monitoring, developmental trajectory
>     - **Related Topics**: Dual Process Theory, Cognitive Load Theory, Self-Regulated Learning, Metacognitive Control, Attention and Self-Regulation

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Selective Attention and Attentional Control
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A focused analytical examination of selective attention mechanisms — the processes by which the cognitive system prioritizes certain inputs while suppressing others — spanning Broadbent's early-selection filter model, Treisman's attenuation theory, and Lavie's perceptual load theory. The report analytically weighs the evidence for early versus late selection, examines voluntary (top-down) and involuntary (bottom-up) attentional control, and explores implications for learning in distraction-rich environments, multimedia design, and the attentional blink phenomenon. The analysis grounds attentional control within the broader executive function framework and connects to metacognitive monitoring during study.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Early vs. late selection, filter theory, attenuation model, perceptual load, top-down vs. bottom-up attention, attentional blink, inattentional blindness, mind-wandering, goal-directed attention
>     - **Related Topics**: Executive Function and Cognitive Control, Cognitive Load Theory, Sustained Attention and Vigilance, Metacognitive Monitoring, Dual Process Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Analogical Reasoning ✅
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on analogical reasoning — the cognitive process of mapping structural relations from a source domain onto a target domain to support inference, problem-solving, and learning. The report traces the empirical and theoretical tradition from Spearman through Gentner's structure-mapping theory, examining what makes analogies more or less successful, the developmental arc of analogical ability, and the role of relational knowledge in enabling cross-domain transfer. Applications in science education, mathematical reasoning, case-based learning, and creative discovery are examined alongside the limits and failure modes of analogical reasoning.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Structure-mapping, source and target domain, relational similarity, surface vs. structural alignment, systematic relations, analogical inference, schema induction, near and far analogies
>     - **Related Topics**: Transfer of Learning, Mental Models (Johnson-Laird), Schema Theory, Abductive Reasoning, Case-Based Learning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Mental Models (Johnson-Laird) ✅
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive foundational treatment of Johnson-Laird's mental models theory — the proposal that human reasoning operates by constructing, inspecting, and revising internal representations that are analogically structured to the situations they represent, rather than through formal rule application. The report surveys the theory's development from Johnson-Laird's syllogistic reasoning studies through its extension to spatial, temporal, and causal reasoning, examines how mental models support both valid inference and predictable error, and traces applications in instruction (particularly science education), text comprehension, and intelligent tutoring system design.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Situation models, model-based reasoning, analogical representation, syllogistic reasoning errors, spatial models, mental simulation, counterfactual reasoning, model revision
>     - **Related Topics**: Analogical Reasoning, Schema Theory, Causal Reasoning, Systems Thinking, Constructivist Learning Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Mental Models (Johnson-Laird) ✅
>   - **Report Type**: First Principles
>     - **Description**: A first-principles inquiry into the mental models framework that interrogates its central claim: that reasoning is fundamentally model-based rather than rule-based. The analysis audits the assumption that internal representations are analogically structured to external reality, examines the tension between mental models and propositional accounts of reasoning, and probes whether the evidence for model-based reasoning is distinguishable from evidence for schema-based processing. The reconstruction derives axioms about the nature of representations that must be true for reasoning errors to take the systematic forms they do, yielding a revised account of when model-based and rule-based processes interact.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Analogical vs. propositional representation, model individuation problem, reasoning error systematicity, rule vs. model controversy, simulation hypothesis, format of mental representation
>     - **Related Topics**: Dual Process Theory, Schema Theory, Cognitive Science foundations, Philosophy of Mind, Analogical Reasoning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Causal Reasoning
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: An analytical examination of causal reasoning — how humans infer, represent, and use causal relationships — drawing on Pearl's do-calculus, the power PC theory (Cheng), and Bayesian causal models. The report analytically contrasts associative and interventionist accounts of causation, examines the developmental emergence of causal reasoning in children, and explores how causal model representations support counterfactual thinking, prediction, and action planning. Particular attention is paid to the conditions under which causal reasoning succeeds or fails, including confounding, temporal distance, and mechanistic versus covariation cues.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Interventionist causation, covariation vs. mechanism cues, causal Bayes nets, counterfactual conditionals, power PC theory, causal induction, confounding, temporal contiguity, causal model revision
>     - **Related Topics**: Counterfactual Thinking, Abductive Reasoning, Systems Thinking, Bayesian Reasoning, Hypothesis Testing and Falsification

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Inductive and Deductive Reasoning
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on the twin engines of formal and informal reasoning — induction and deduction — covering their logical structures, epistemic properties, and psychological realities. The report distinguishes the truth-preserving but content-limited nature of deduction from the ampliative but inherently fallible character of induction, surveys the psychological literature on syllogistic reasoning errors, the confirmation bias in hypothesis testing, and the conditions that facilitate stronger or weaker inductive conclusions. The philosophical problem of induction (Hume), Goodman's new riddle, and Bayesian responses are addressed alongside the teaching of reasoning skills.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Validity and soundness, syllogism, modus ponens, modus tollens, inductive strength, confirmation bias, base rate neglect, Wason selection task, Bayesian updating, ampliative reasoning
>     - **Related Topics**: Dual Process Theory, Abductive Reasoning, Bayesian Reasoning, Hypothesis Testing and Falsification, Critical Thinking

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Extended Mind Theory ✅
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on Clark and Chalmers's extended mind thesis — the claim that cognitive processes can constitutively extend beyond skin and skull into tools, notebooks, and environments when those external resources satisfy the functional criteria for cognitive states. The report traces the parity principle argument, examines the supporting case studies (Otto's notebook, Tetris rotation experiments), surveys the philosophical and cognitive science literature that has developed around this thesis, and explores its radical implications for personal identity, memory, and the design of cognitive tools including personal knowledge management systems.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Parity principle, cognitive constitution, Otto's notebook, coupling-constitution fallacy objection, active externalism, offloaded cognition, situated cognitive systems, cognitive bloat objection
>     - **Related Topics**: Embodied Cognition, Distributed Cognition, 4E Cognition, Personal Knowledge Management, Building a Personal Knowledge Base with Obsidian

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Extended Mind Theory
>   - **Report Type**: First Principles
>     - **Description**: A first-principles audit of the extended mind thesis that examines the parity principle from the ground up — asking what functional criteria are genuinely sufficient for cognitive constitution, and whether Clark and Chalmers's parity argument smuggles in assumptions about representation and accessibility that are more demanding than acknowledged. The analysis deconstructs the coupling-constitution distinction, interrogates whether the thesis requires a commitment to functionalism about mental states, and examines the implications of accepting extended cognition for epistemic responsibility, knowledge attribution, and the metaphysics of the mind. Reconstruction derives a minimal set of conditions for legitimate cognitive extension.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Parity principle scrutiny, functional equivalence conditions, coupling vs. constitution, functionalism requirement, epistemic implications, cognitive bloat, active externalism vs. internalism, representation debate
>     - **Related Topics**: Embodied Cognition, 4E Cognition, Philosophy of Mind, Functionalism and Computationalism, Distributed Cognition

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Embodied Cognition
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive foundational treatment of embodied cognition — the research program holding that the body's sensorimotor systems are not merely instruments of mind but constitutive participants in cognition itself. The report maps the major theoretical positions from Merleau-Ponty's phenomenological grounding through Varela, Thompson, and Rosch's enactivism and Barsalou's grounded cognition theory, surveys the empirical evidence from motor-language interactions, physical manipulation effects on abstract thought, and embodied metaphor research, and examines the educational implications for learning through action, gesture, and physical experience.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Sensorimotor grounding, perceptual simulation, embodied metaphor (Lakoff & Johnson), motor-language coupling, grounded cognition, bodily schema, affordances (Gibson), proprioception in learning, action-perception loops
>     - **Related Topics**: 4E Cognition, Enactivism, Extended Mind Theory, Situated Cognition, Gesture and Learning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: 4E Cognition (Embodied, Embedded, Enacted, Extended)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: An analytical examination of the 4E cognition framework as an integrative umbrella that consolidates four distinct but related challenges to the classical information-processing view: embodiment (the body shapes cognition), embeddedness (cognition is scaffolded by environment), enaction (cognition is constituted by sense-making activity), and extension (cognition can include external resources). The report analytically distinguishes the specific claims of each E, examines where they converge and where they generate genuine theoretical tension, and evaluates the explanatory added value of treating them as a unified research program versus as separate theoretical commitments with distinct empirical bases.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Embodiment, embeddedness, enaction, extension, autopoiesis, sense-making, scaffold-dependency, functional cognitive system, cognitive continuity, anti-representationalism
>     - **Related Topics**: Embodied Cognition, Extended Mind Theory, Enactivism, Situated Cognition, Distributed Cognition

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Constructivist Learning Theory
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive foundational report on constructivism — the family of learning theories holding that knowledge is actively constructed by learners through assimilation and accommodation rather than passively received from instruction. The report covers the Piagetian cognitive-developmental tradition, von Glasersfeld's radical constructivism, and social constructivist extensions (Vygotsky), maps the empirical bases for constructivist principles, surveys instructional models derived from them (problem-based learning, inquiry-based learning, project-based learning), and engages honestly with critiques from cognitive load and direct instruction researchers. The tensions between constructivism as epistemology and as pedagogy are clearly articulated.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Assimilation, accommodation, equilibration, zone of proximal development, active knowledge construction, discovery learning, authentic tasks, cognitive conflict, social mediation, prior knowledge
>     - **Related Topics**: Piaget's developmental theory, Sociocultural Theory, Zone of Proximal Development, Problem-Based Learning, Direct Instruction vs. Discovery Learning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Constructivist Learning Theory
>   - **Report Type**: First Principles
>     - **Description**: A first-principles interrogation of constructivism that audits its foundational epistemological claim — that all knowledge is personally constructed — and examines what pedagogical conclusions can and cannot legitimately follow from it. The analysis identifies the conflation of constructivism as epistemology (all knowledge is actively interpreted) with constructivism as instructional method (therefore learners must discover rather than receive knowledge), argues this is a non-sequitur, and reconstructs from first principles what the genuine implications of active meaning-making are for instruction. The report examines where discovery-based approaches are empirically well-supported and where direct instruction evidence challenges constructivist pedagogical prescriptions.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Constructivism as epistemology vs. pedagogy, knowledge construction fallacy, novice-expert distinction, discovery learning evidence, direct instruction debate, guided vs. unguided learning, meaningful reception learning
>     - **Related Topics**: Cognitive Load Theory, Direct Instruction vs. Discovery Learning, Productive Failure, Guided Discovery, Schema Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Generative Learning Theory (Wittrock)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A focused analytical treatment of Merlin Wittrock's generative learning model — the proposal that meaningful learning requires learners to actively generate connections between new information and prior knowledge, between parts of the text, and between text and context. The report examines the theoretical architecture (generative model of the brain's information processing), surveys the foundational empirical studies on generative activities (summarization, question generation, analogy creation), and traces the theory's influence on Mayer's generative theory of multimedia learning and the broader active learning literature. The analysis evaluates the generative processing construct's explanatory precision and distinguishes it from related engagement constructs.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Generative processing, connection-making, elaborative integration, organizational processing, active learning processes, prior knowledge relationships, meaningful learning, constructive activity
>     - **Related Topics**: Dual Coding Theory, Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning, Self-Explanation, Elaborative Interrogation, Active Learning Strategies

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Sociocultural Theory (Vygotsky)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on Vygotsky's sociocultural theory of cognitive development — arguably the most influential alternative to Piagetian developmental psychology — examining its core propositions: that higher mental functions originate in social interaction, that language is the primary tool of thought, that all learning involves the internalization of culturally mediated activity, and that development proceeds from the interpsychological to the intrapsychological plane. The report traces the theory's historical context in Soviet psychology, covers the zone of proximal development, scaffolding, private speech, and the semiotic mediation framework, and surveys its influence on literacy education, collaborative learning, and cultural-historical activity theory.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Zone of proximal development, semiotic mediation, internalization, private speech, interpsychological to intrapsychological, cultural tools, scaffolding, more knowledgeable other, social origins of mind
>     - **Related Topics**: Zone of Proximal Development, Scaffolding in Learning, Cognitive Apprenticeship, Activity Theory, Constructivist Learning Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Zone of Proximal Development
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A precise analytical investigation into Vygotsky's Zone of Proximal Development (ZPD) — the gap between what a learner can achieve independently and what they can achieve with appropriate guidance — examining the concept's original formulation, its subsequent elaboration and operationalization challenges, and the scaffolding literature it inspired. The analysis distinguishes Vygotsky's dynamic assessment applications of the ZPD from its instructional applications, examines the evidence for and against the concept's unique predictive power beyond IQ measures, and evaluates how the metaphor of a "zone" may over-simplify what is a dynamic and relational developmental process.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Dynamic assessment, scaffolding, instructional assistance, independent vs. assisted performance, developmental potential, contingent teaching, peer collaboration, calibrated challenge
>     - **Related Topics**: Scaffolding in Learning, Cognitive Apprenticeship, Sociocultural Theory, Feedback in Learning, Formative Assessment

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Social Learning Theory (Bandura)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational survey of Albert Bandura's social learning theory and its evolution into social cognitive theory — examining the central mechanisms of observational learning (attention, retention, motor reproduction, motivation), the concept of reciprocal determinism, the emergence of self-efficacy as a central construct, and the theory's account of how behavior is regulated through self-observation, self-judgment, and self-reaction. The report traces the landmark Bobo doll studies through to vicarious reinforcement, symbolic modeling, and the self-regulatory applications that distinguish social cognitive theory from behaviorist predecessors.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Observational learning, modeling, vicarious reinforcement, reciprocal determinism, self-efficacy, self-regulation, symbolic modeling, attention in observational learning, outcome expectancies
>     - **Related Topics**: Self-Efficacy Theory, Self-Regulated Learning, Behavioral Learning Theory, Cognitive Apprenticeship, Motivation

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Communities of Practice (Wenger)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A focused analytical examination of Wenger's communities of practice (CoP) framework — the theory that learning is fundamentally a process of social participation in communities organized around shared practices, and that identity, meaning, and competence are negotiated within and through membership in such communities. The analysis examines the three key structural dimensions of CoPs (mutual engagement, joint enterprise, shared repertoire), evaluates the concept of legitimate peripheral participation as a theory of newcomer development, and critically assesses the CoP framework's application to organizational learning, professional development, and online learning communities, including its limitations for formal educational contexts.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Mutual engagement, joint enterprise, shared repertoire, legitimate peripheral participation, identity formation, boundary crossing, participation vs. reification, brokering, learning trajectory
>     - **Related Topics**: Situated Learning Theory, Legitimate Peripheral Participation, Activity Theory, Cognitive Apprenticeship, Professional Development

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Experiential Learning Theory (Kolb)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on David Kolb's experiential learning theory — grounded in Dewey, Lewin, and Piaget — that presents learning as a cyclical process moving through concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualization, and active experimentation. The report explains the theoretical architecture, surveys the empirical evidence for the learning cycle and the associated Learning Style Inventory (LSI), examines critiques of the learning styles component while affirming the cycle's structural validity, and explores applications in professional education, outdoor learning, workplace training, and reflective practice frameworks. Kolb's place within the broader experiential and constructivist traditions is clearly mapped.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Concrete experience, reflective observation, abstract conceptualization, active experimentation, learning cycle, experiential learning space, learning flexibility, integration of dialectics, grasping vs. transforming
>     - **Related Topics**: Reflective Practice (Schön), Transformative Learning Theory, Problem-Based Learning, Andragogy, Service Learning

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Transformative Learning Theory (Mezirow)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: An analytical examination of Mezirow's transformative learning theory — the account of how disorienting dilemmas trigger a process of critical reflection on underlying assumptions (frames of reference), dialogue with others, and ultimately a revised worldview that alters habitual interpretations. The report analytically dissects the phases of transformative learning, evaluates the role of rational discourse in legitimating perspective transformation, and examines challenges to Mezirow's primarily rational-linguistic model from Boyd's extrarational traditions and Dirkx's emphasis on imagination and emotion. Empirical attempts to operationalize and measure perspective transformation are critically assessed.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Frame of reference, meaning scheme, meaning perspective, disorienting dilemma, critical reflection, perspective transformation, rational discourse, point of view, habit of mind
>     - **Related Topics**: Critical Reflection in Adult Learning, Reflective Practice (Schön), Experiential Learning Theory, Andragogy, Epistemic Humility

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Reflective Practice (Schön)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational treatment of Donald Schön's reflective practice framework — developed in The Reflective Practitioner as a challenge to technical-rationality models of professional knowledge — examining the distinction between reflection-in-action and reflection-on-action, the concept of knowing-in-action, and how practitioners frame problems through the lens of prior cases (repertoire). The report explores the implications of Schön's epistemology of practice for professional education across fields (architecture, medicine, education, management), surveys the subsequent development of reflective practice models, and addresses the conceptual challenges of developing reflective competence in training.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Technical rationality, reflection-in-action, reflection-on-action, knowing-in-action, reframing, repertoire, problem setting, swampy lowlands of practice, espoused theory vs. theory-in-use
>     - **Related Topics**: Transformative Learning Theory, Experiential Learning Theory, Critical Reflection in Adult Learning, Professional Development, Metacognition

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Connectivism (Siemens & Downes)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on connectivism — George Siemens and Stephen Downes's learning theory for the digital age, which proposes that learning consists in the formation and traversal of connections across networks of nodes (people, organizations, databases) rather than in the accumulation of knowledge within an individual mind. The report examines the core principles (learning can reside in non-human appliances, currency of knowledge matters, decision-making is a learning process), evaluates the debate over whether connectivism constitutes a genuinely new theory or an application of existing network and complexity theories, and explores its implications for MOOCs, personal learning networks, and knowledge management in information-saturated environments.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Network learning, node and connection, chaos and complexity theory, pipe maintenance, current knowledge, distributed knowledge, pattern recognition, personal learning network, wayfinding
>     - **Related Topics**: Networked Learning, Personal Learning Environments, Personal Knowledge Management, Rhizomatic Learning, Self-Organized Learning Environments

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning (CTML)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: An analytical examination of Mayer's Cognitive Theory of Multimedia Learning — grounded in dual coding theory and cognitive load theory — that maps the twelve empirically-derived design principles (coherence, signaling, redundancy, spatial contiguity, temporal contiguity, segmenting, pre-training, modality, multimedia, personalization, voice, image effects) and traces the experimental evidence behind each. The report analytically assesses the boundary conditions and moderators for each principle, examines where learner characteristics (prior knowledge, spatial ability) reverse or attenuate predicted effects, and evaluates CTML's practical application to e-learning, video instruction, and textbook design.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Dual channel assumption, limited capacity, active processing, coherence effect, modality effect, multimedia principle, segmenting, pre-training, generative and essential processing, learner-appropriate media
>     - **Related Topics**: Dual Coding Theory, Cognitive Load Theory, Multimedia Learning Theory, Instructional Design, Sketch-Noting and Visual Note-Taking

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Flow Theory (Csikszentmihalyi)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational treatment of Csikszentmihalyi's flow theory — the phenomenological and empirical account of optimal experience characterized by complete absorption, merging of action and awareness, effortlessness, loss of self-consciousness, and intrinsic motivation. The report examines the challenge-skills balance as the primary condition for flow onset, surveys the methodology of experience sampling research that grounds the theory empirically, explores flow's relationship to intrinsic motivation, creativity, and well-being, and examines its application in educational design, game-based learning, athletic performance, and professional environments. The theory's phenomenological limitations and operationalization challenges are addressed.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Challenge-skills balance, absorption, merging of action and awareness, autotelic experience, loss of self-consciousness, clear goals, unambiguous feedback, experience sampling method, optimal experience
>     - **Related Topics**: Intrinsic vs. Extrinsic Motivation, Self-Determination Theory, Creativity Theories, Game-Based Learning, Interest Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Self-Efficacy Theory (Bandura)
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A foundational report on Bandura's self-efficacy construct — the belief in one's capacity to execute the courses of action required to produce specific outcomes — examining its four information sources (mastery experiences, vicarious learning, verbal persuasion, physiological states), its domain-specificity, its relationship to outcome expectations, and its influence on goal-setting, persistence, effort regulation, and academic performance. The report surveys the extensive empirical literature across academic, health, vocational, and athletic domains, examines the distinction between self-efficacy and related constructs (self-concept, self-esteem, perceived competence), and addresses the conditions under which self-efficacy interventions are most effective.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Mastery experiences, vicarious reinforcement, verbal persuasion, physiological arousal interpretation, domain-specificity, goal-setting, persistence, outcome expectations, calibration of self-efficacy
>     - **Related Topics**: Social Learning Theory (Bandura), Growth Mindset, Self-Regulated Learning, Attribution Theory, Expectancy-Value Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Intrinsic vs. Extrinsic Motivation
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: An analytical examination of the intrinsic-extrinsic motivation distinction — its theoretical foundations in cognitive evaluation theory, the overjustification effect research (Lepper, Deci), and decades of debate about when and why external rewards undermine intrinsic motivation. The report analytically evaluates the meta-analytic evidence landscape, distinguishes between tangible and verbal rewards, expected and unexpected rewards, and contingent and non-contingent delivery, examining under what precise conditions external incentives enhance or harm intrinsic engagement. The analysis addresses the practical implications for educational reward systems, gamification, and the design of competency-based environments.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Overjustification effect, cognitive evaluation theory, reward contingency, verbal vs. tangible rewards, informational vs. controlling feedback, engagement quality, autonomous motivation, identified regulation
>     - **Related Topics**: Self-Determination Theory, Cognitive Evaluation Theory, Flow Theory, Goal Orientation Theory, Achievement Goal Theory

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Self-Determination Theory ✅
>   - **Report Type**: Foundational
>     - **Description**: A comprehensive foundational report on Deci and Ryan's Self-Determination Theory (SDT) — one of the most empirically productive macro-theories in motivational psychology — spanning all six mini-theories: Cognitive Evaluation Theory (CET), Organismic Integration Theory (OIT), Causality Orientations Theory (COT), Basic Psychological Needs Theory (BPNT), Goal Contents Theory (GCT), and Relationship Motivation Theory (RMT). The report examines the core constructs of basic psychological needs (competence, autonomy, relatedness), the internalization continuum from external to identified to integrated regulation, and the extensive empirical literature across education, healthcare, sport, and organizational settings documenting the effects of need satisfaction and need thwarting on well-being, engagement, and performance.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Basic psychological needs, autonomy, competence, relatedness, self-determination continuum, internalization, need satisfaction and thwarting, autonomous vs. controlled motivation, intrinsic motivation, organismic dialectical perspective
>     - **Related Topics**: Intrinsic vs. Extrinsic Motivation, Flow Theory, Self-Efficacy Theory, Goal Orientation Theory, Autonomy Support in Teaching

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Self-Determination Theory ✅
>   - **Report Type**: First Principles
>     - **Description**: A first-principles interrogation of Self-Determination Theory that audits the foundational claim that human beings have three universal, innate basic psychological needs for autonomy, competence, and relatedness — examining the philosophical debt to organismic and humanistic traditions, the challenges of operationalizing "basic needs" empirically, and whether cross-cultural universality evidence is sufficient to support the innate claim. The analysis also examines the conceptual boundary between autonomy (self-determination) and agency, and interrogates whether the internalization continuum is truly a spectrum of self-determination or whether identified and integrated regulation are phenomenologically distinct from intrinsic motivation. A reconstructed framework is derived from minimal axioms about goal pursuit and need satisfaction.
>     - **Core Concepts**: Basic needs universality claim, innateness vs. socialized needs, autonomy-agency distinction, internalization continuum scrutiny, cross-cultural evidence, organismic philosophy, humanistic psychology roots, needs vs. desires
>     - **Related Topics**: Intrinsic vs. Extrinsic Motivation, Humanistic Learning Theory, Maslow's Hierarchy critique, Growth Mindset, Philosophy of Agency

> [!topic-idea] From: [[report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11|report-generation-topic-inventory-by-style-2026-03-11]]
> - **Topic**: Growth Mindset (Dweck)
>   - **Report Type**: Focused Analysis
>     - **Description**: A focused analytical examination of Carol Dweck's implicit theories of intelligence framework — contrasting entity (fixed) and incremental (growth) theories of ability — surveying the experimental evidence for mindset effects on challenge-seeking, persistence, and performance, and engaging critically with the replication literature including the large pre-registered studies (Yeager et al.) that found smaller-than-expected effects in school interventions.
